> AGENT

Good instinct on both — let me map each against what's actually written.

## The two styles: already stated

The "single shared ring vs. N rings polled by one consumer" split, *and* the "no single winner, we'd have both" conclusion, are both explicit — in `### Fan-in (composition, not a mode)` (design doc line 506):

> "The other standard many-to-one shape: one SPSC ring per producer, the consumer polls N endpoints. **Both have a place**" ... "likely both shapes get offered eventually."

And it's not just asserted — the measurement plan (line 525) benches them head-to-head at matching producer counts and explicitly refuses to pick a winner up front: *"The matrix exists to check that, not assume it."* The current guess there is shared-ring loses on throughput under contention, wins on consumer-side latency/fairness at large N. So point 1 is captured, including your "probably both" hunch.

## The general-connectivity principle: only implicit

This one you're right to flag — it is **not** stated as a principle. What exists are three *facets* of it, scattered:

- **Consumer with in-degree > 1** — fan-in (one consumer, N rings). Covered.
- **A node that both receives and sends** — System model line 621: *"alloc → write → send the descriptor over a queue → the receiver owns it → read, **forward over another queue**, or free."* That's one entity holding a consume endpoint and a produce endpoint — a 2-hop pipeline — but stated as a lifecycle step, not a topology rule.
- **Message mobility** — line 605: *"Any message travels over any queue."*

What's missing is the step back that ties them together: **the ring/queue is an *edge*; an entity is a *node* that may hold any number of endpoints, in either role, at once** — consume ring A while producing on B and C. Connections form an arbitrary directed graph, not just 1:1 links or many-to-one stars. And note one corner isn't even implied: **fan-out from a single producer** (one entity, multiple outbound edges) appears nowhere — every current shape is many-to-*one*.

So: the two edge-flavors are well-covered; the node-degree / graph framing above them is the gap.

## Proposed statement

I'd add it as a top-level principle bullet in `### System model (overview)` (the "rules at a glance" list is exactly the right altitude), something like:

> - **Entities form an arbitrary directed graph** [details](#…).
>   - A queue/ring is an *edge*; an entity (thread or process) is a *node* that may hold any number of endpoints, in either role, at once — consume ring A while producing on B and C.
>   - The two many-to-one realizations (shared MPSC ring, fan-in of SPSC rings) are two edge-shapes for the same consumer in-degree; neither is privileged.
>   - Fan-out (one producer, many edges) is equally admissible — nothing in the primitives assumes an entity has degree 1.

That also feeds the chores-02 refactoring: if `spsc`/`mpsc` are sibling *edge* primitives and composition (fan-in/fan-out) is a node-level concern, it reinforces keeping the two ring flavors as peer modules and leaving composition helpers out of them.

Want me to add that bullet to the System model overview (and maybe a one-line echo in the ring-buffer `## Goal` so the graph framing is visible up top)? Or would you rather it live somewhere else — a short dedicated `### Topology` subsection?

> AGENT

Yes — we agree on the substance, and let me state it precisely so there's no daylight between us:

**A single thread can simultaneously hold:**
- `Producer`/`Consumer` handles for any number of SPSC rings,
- `MpscProducer`/`MpscConsumer` handles for any number of MPSC rings,
- the owning-allocator role for several pools, and free into any pool it can reach.

Nothing caps the degree. These handles are just owned values — a thread holds as many as it likes; the endpoint and pool contracts constrain *how each single ring/pool is used* (one producer per SPSC ring, one allocator per pool), never *how many* a thread participates in. So a thread is a hub, not a leaf.

**On the granularity you flagged** — you're right that the bullet pins "entity" to thread/process, and I think that pinning is *correct but incomplete*, for a reason worth being explicit about:

- Thread/process is the **contract** granularity — it's the level at which the guarantees are actually defined: `Send`/`Sync` bounds, per-process shared-memory mapping, "single producer" = one thread driving an SPSC endpoint at a time. So for ownership and safety, thread/process is the right node.
- But the **logical** communicating unit can be *finer* than a thread — an actor/task on a shared executor. "One thread holding N edges" and "N actors sharing one thread, each holding one edge" are indistinguishable at the wiring level. So thread/process is the *realization* granularity, not a claim that a node = exactly one logical role.

Put differently: the design neither requires nor forbids one-endpoint-per-thread. A thread multiplexing many logical roles is exactly the multiplicity you're describing.

**Two small gaps this exposes in the written doc:**
1. The new bullet says "endpoints" and only illustrates rings — it doesn't mention pools. Your statement lumps pools in.
2. Pool multiplicity is stated at *process* granularity ("A process may have one or more pools") but thread→multiple-pools isn't explicit.

So I'd refine the bullet to close both — keeping thread/process as the node, but making the "hub" nature and pools explicit:

> - **Entities form an arbitrary directed graph** [details](#fan-in-composition-not-a-mode).
>   - A queue is an edge; an entity — a thread or process, possibly hosting several logical roles — is a node that may hold endpoints for many rings at once, in either role, and own or free into several pools: consume ring A, produce on B and C, allocate from pool P.
>   - The two many-to-one realizations (shared MPSC ring, fan-in of SPSC rings) are two edge-shapes for the same consumer in-degree; neither is privileged.
>   - Fan-out (one producer, many edges) is equally admissible — nothing in the primitives assumes an entity has degree 1.

Does that match your model — thread/process as the node, explicitly a hub over many rings *and* pools, with "logical roles" acknowledged? If so I'll swap it in.

> AGENT

Sorry — that was jargon dressed up as a point. Let me say it plainly.

I was drawing a line between two things:

- **The logical entity** — the *conceptual* thing that communicates: "the order service," "actor 7," a task. It exists in your design/head.
- **The thread or process** — the *actual running thing* that executes code and physically holds the handles.

By "realization granularity" I just meant: **the thread/process is the concrete unit that actually runs a logical entity** — the logical actor gets "realized" (made real, executed) *by* a thread. Nothing more.

Why it mattered: a logical actor doesn't have to be one-per-thread. One thread can run many actors (think an async executor with 100 tasks on 4 threads). So:

- If you model nodes as **logical actors**, one thread might be several nodes.
- If you model nodes as **threads/processes**, that same thread is one node holding many edges.

Both describe the identical wiring — the difference is only where you draw the box. My clumsy sentence was just: the doc draws the box at thread/process (the thing that runs), and that's fine, because whatever logical structure you layer on top still maps down onto threads and processes.

Frankly it's a distinction that adds more confusion than value here. I'd **drop the "logical roles" hedge entirely** and keep the bullet concrete: an entity is a thread or process, and it can hold endpoints for many rings and own/free into many pools. Want me to go with that simpler version instead of the one with "possibly hosting several logical roles"?

> AGENT

Grepped — ISRs are absent from the whole design corpus. The only hit is line 309, "may WFE on an interrupt," which is the *blocking* layer (a thread parking until an interrupt wakes it), not an ISR *holding an endpoint*. So this is genuinely new ground, and you're correcting a real flattening in my model.

## The corrected model: two axes, not one list

I wrongly put "thread" and "process" in one bag ("the concrete unit"). They're different axes:

- **Execution context** — the thing that actually runs code and drives ring/pool ops. Two kinds, and you're right they're distinct: **thread** and **ISR**. An ISR isn't a thread — it preempts, runs to completion, and can't block. *This* is the execution granularity, and it's what a graph node really is.
- **Process** — not an executor at all. It's an address space grouping one or more execution contexts, and it's the boundary that owns the shared-memory mappings and pool registries. It's a *fence around* nodes, not a node.

So: **nodes are execution contexts (threads/ISRs); rings are edges; a process is a boundary the graph spans.** Crossing a process edge = crossing into another address space (needs shared memory + a registered pool). My "concrete unit" line conflated the executor with the fence.

## Why this matters here (not just pedantry)

ISR-as-endpoint is a first-class case for *this* library specifically, and it has teeth:

- **Lock-free is exactly what makes ISR endpoints viable** — SPSC's load/store (and MPSC's CAS) hold no lock across any point, so an ISR can produce/consume without the classic "ISR grabbed a mutex the thread holds" deadlock.
- **The non-blocking probe is the ISR wait policy** — an ISR must not spin unbounded, so `on_full`/`on_empty` = `|_| false` (single probe, bail on Full/Empty) is precisely the ISR-appropriate policy. The wait-policy seam already accommodates it.
- **"Single producer" must mean single *context*, and preemption sharpens it** — if a thread *and* an ISR both produce on one SPSC ring, that's two producers; worse, the ISR preempts the thread mid-`reserve`/`commit` on the same core → protocol violation. I'm confident the fix is: thread + ISR feeding one ring ⇒ use MPSC, whose CAS-claim tolerates the preemptive interleave (the ISR just claims a different slot; the thread resumes and commits). That's a genuinely useful rule the doc doesn't state.

## Proposed bullet (revised)

> - **Entities form an arbitrary directed graph** [details](#fan-in-composition-not-a-mode).
>   - A ring is an edge; a node is an *execution context* — a thread or an ISR — that may hold endpoints for many rings at once, in either role, and own or free into several pools: consume ring A, produce on B and C, allocate from pool P.
>   - A *process* is not a node but a boundary — an address space grouping one or more execution contexts and owning the pool mappings and registries; an edge crossing it crosses into another address space (shared memory + a registered pool).
>   - The two many-to-one realizations (shared MPSC ring, fan-in of SPSC rings) are two edge-shapes for the same consumer in-degree; neither is privileged.
>   - Fan-out (one producer, many edges) is equally admissible — nothing in the primitives assumes a node has degree 1.

Two questions before I write it in:

1. Does that two-axis framing (node = thread/ISR execution context; process = boundary) match how you see it?
2. The "single producer = single context; thread + ISR ⇒ MPSC" rule feels worth capturing too, but it's a correctness note, not a topology-overview line. Want it as a separate short note (a `### Execution contexts` subsection, or a Constraints bullet), or leave it out of the doc for now?

82	## Constraints
83	
84	- **Slot geometry: M slots × N bytes** — the ring holds M
85	  [slots](#terminology) of N bytes each.
86	  - M (capacity) is a power of two — index wrapping is a
87	    mask, no division; checked at construction.
88	  - N (slot size) is a multiple of the cache-line size, and
89	    each slot is cache-line aligned — adjacent slots never
90	    share a line, and any `T` with `align_of::<T>()` up to
91	    the line size fits without padding logic.
92	  - This trades space for isolation (a 16-byte message
93	    occupies a full 64-byte slot) — the right default for
94	    IPC; a packed-slot variant is a possible follow-on.
95	- **Monotonic indices** — producer/consumer indices are
96	  free-running unsigned counters (wrap via masking), so
97	  full/empty are distinguishable without a separate flag or
98	  a sacrificial slot.
99	- **Cache-line separation** — producer- and consumer-owned
100	  indices are padded/aligned to separate cache lines to avoid
101	  false sharing.
102	- **Typed access over untyped slots** — the slot geometry
103	  (M, N) is a property of the buffer, independent of any
104	  message type; a `T` is a zerocopy view into a slot, valid
105	  when `size_of::<T>() <= N` and `align_of::<T>()` divides
106	  the slot alignment (checked at construction or compile
107	  time). Variable-length framed messages are a possible
108	  follow-on, not in this cycle.
109	- **zerocopy, core-only** — depend on zerocopy with default
110	  features compatible with `no_std`; no other runtime
111	  dependencies.
112	
113	## Memory layout
```

> - **Cache-line separation** — producer- and consumer-owned indices are padded/aligned to separate cache lines to avoid false sharing.
> - **Endpoint roles bind to one execution context** — an SPSC ring's producer role (and its consumer role) is one thread *or* one ISR, never shared across contexts, never "a process."
>   - Two contexts on one SPSC role race it: an ISR that preempts a producer thread mid-`reserve`/`commit` on the same core is a second producer.
>   - When several contexts must feed one ring — an ISR and a thread, say — that is the MPSC ring: its CAS claim tolerates the preemptive interleave (the ISR claims a different slot; the preempted thread resumes and commits).
> - **Typed access over untyped slots** — …

I placed it right after **Cache-line separation** since both concern the producer/consumer index contract. It leads with the rule (role = one execution context), then the failure it rules out, then the MPSC escape hatch.

The System-model overview bullet is also swapped to the two-axis version (node = execution context thread/ISR; process = boundary).

A couple of things to weigh:
- **Term consistency** — the Constraints bullet says "role," the overview says "node/endpoint." Both fine, but if you want one vocabulary I can align them.

> DEVELOPER

I'm not sure I agree, if the SP side of an SPSC "queue" has a mutex controlling it's shared use I think is can be shared by multiple threads, couldn't it?

> AGENT

You're right — my bullet overstated it. The SPSC contract isn't about *thread identity*; it's about **no concurrent access to the endpoint**. A mutex serializes access, so any number of threads can share the single-producer role by taking turns — at each instant only the lock-holder is in the producer path, which is exactly the invariant SPSC needs. And it's Rust-expressible: `Producer: Send`, so `Arc<Mutex<Producer>>` shares fine; the guard hands you the `&mut Producer` to `reserve`/`commit`.

So the real constraint is *serialization*, not "one thread." Where your mutex intuition **does** hit a wall is the ISR case, and for a specific reason: an ISR can't take that mutex. It preempts the thread that holds the lock and can't block waiting for it (deadlock / priority inversion). So ISR+thread on one endpoint is genuinely concurrent, unserializable-by-lock access — that's the case that forces lock-free MPSC. Two different reasons collapsed into my one bad sentence.

Corrected bullet — I've swapped it in:

> - **SPSC roles forbid concurrent access, not multiple owners** — the single-producer (and single-consumer) contract is "at most one accessor in the endpoint at a time," a *serialization* requirement, not a fixed thread identity.
>   - One thread trivially satisfies it; so do several threads taking turns under a mutex — the lock guarantees no two are in the producer path at once, at the cost of blocking (no longer lock-free).
>   - An ISR is the case a lock cannot cover: it preempts and must not block on a thread-held lock (deadlock / priority inversion), so an ISR sharing a role with a thread is genuine concurrent access — a violation.
>   - Need truly concurrent producers, or a thread + ISR on one ring? Use the MPSC ring — its lock-free CAS claim serializes without a lock.

> AGENT

Agreed — all of it. Let me state the mechanics precisely so we're locked:

- **Consumer is symmetric** — yes, a mutex shares the single-consumer role across threads exactly as it does the producer. My wording was producer-flavored; fixing that.
- **ISRs *can* share, via try-and-bail** — right: an ISR may participate only if it **tests** the flag and, if taken, **abandons** (defers/drops) rather than spinning. Never block, never wait.
- **The real enabler is interrupt-disable** — this is the crux. A same-core ISR can't be serialized by a lock (it can't wait on it), so the *only* way to make the thread's multi-step endpoint access atomic against that ISR is for the thread to run it with interrupts disabled — the `spin_lock_irqsave` pattern. The mutex/flag handles thread-vs-thread; the irq-disable handles thread-vs-ISR. Together they cover both.
- **So the default rule stands** — an ISR *should* be the sole owner of any endpoint it touches; sharing is possible but is the "special care" path, and it hinges on the thread being able to disable interrupts.

That also corrects my previous bullet, which called thread+ISR sharing a flat "violation" — it's a violation *only without* the irqsave guard. Reworked and swapped in:

> AGENT

On **line 136** I think we've got a terminology mismatch, not a technical one — let me make my case before I touch it.

"Sole owner" there means the ISR exclusively owns **one endpoint**, *not* the whole ring. A ring is two endpoints; "ISR sole owner" is the mirror of "Thread sole owner" one bullet up — it's about a single endpoint having a single, unshared owner. The **partner is the other endpoint**, owned by someone else.

So the canonical embedded case *is* exactly this, and it needs **no** interrupt-disable:

- ISR owns the **producer** endpoint (sole owner), a thread owns the **consumer** endpoint (sole owner). Real partners — just on opposite ends of the ring.
- No irqsave required, because producer and consumer touch **disjoint** state: the producer writes `producer_idx` + the slot it's filling; the consumer writes `consumer_idx` + the slot it's reading; the cross-reads (full/empty checks) are single atomic loads. The ISR preempting its partner thread mid-op is just one more interleaving the lock-free SPSC handoff already tolerates (the same one it tolerates across two cores).
- Interrupt-disable only enters when the ISR **shares one endpoint** with a thread — i.e. both produce on the same ring. That's the *next* bullet ("ISR sharing a role with a thread"), and there it's genuinely required.

So your instinct "a ring without a partner is useless" is right — but "ISR sole owner" never meant a partnerless ring; it meant the ISR doesn't *share its end*. The partner sits on the other end.

Where I think the bullet failed you is that "sole owner" reads as "owns the ring alone." Proposed rewrite to kill that:

> - **ISR, sole owner of an endpoint** — the canonical embedded ring: the ISR exclusively owns *one* end (say the producer), a thread owns the other.
>   - No interrupt disable needed — producer and consumer touch disjoint state, so the ISR preempting its partner is just another interleaving the lock-free handoff tolerates.
>   - Why *sole* ownership: an ISR can't be serialized by a lock (it preempts, can't block on a thread-held one), so it must never *share* an endpoint — only own one outright.

Does that reasoning land? If so I'll swap it in; if you're picturing a case where the ISR-owned endpoint really does need interrupt-disable, walk me through it — I may be missing your scenario.

> DEVELOPER

But they are in common sturctures and if any field is written by the ISR it must not be writable by the other side, and of course, the access must be atomic.

> AGENT

Agreed — and that's the precise precondition, better than my loose "disjoint state." What makes preemption safe isn't that the two sides touch unrelated memory (they share the header and slots), it's that within those common structures:

1. **Single writer per field** — a field the ISR writes (its index, its in-flight slot) is read-only to the partner; no field has two writers.
2. **Atomic access** — the shared control words are read/written atomically, so no torn value crosses the preemption point.

That's exactly what the SPSC protocol already enforces (`producer_idx` written only by the producer, `consumer_idx` only by the consumer, both `AtomicU32`; the slot payload single-writer and published through the index handoff). So the "no irqsave" claim *depends* on those two conditions holding — which is worth saying, not assuming.

Folding that in — rewritten with the two preconditions as sub-sub-bullets (keeping your no-paragraphs preference):

> AGENT

Consistency done in the subsection (both headers now "…of an endpoint" / "…an endpoint", and "role"→"endpoint" throughout). One holdover: the **Constraints bullet title** still says "SPSC **roles** forbid concurrent access" — that's the *category* usage ("the producer/consumer roles"), which reads fine, but say the word and I'll make it "endpoints" too.

## The all-ISR system: yes, and it's arguably the sweet spot

Short answer: **yes** — N producing ISRs → 1 consuming ISR works today on a single MPSC ring, and N:M works as composition. And it's not a stretch of the design; lock-free rings are *exactly* what ISR-to-ISR wants. Here's the reasoning and the real constraints.

**Why it works at all** — the ring protocols never block and never hold a lock:
- SPSC is load/store, MPSC is CAS. An ISR can execute either — bounded, non-blocking work.
- **No priority inversion is even possible** — there's no lock for a low-priority ISR to hold hostage, so a high-priority ISR never waits on a low one. That's the failure mode that normally makes ISR IPC scary, and it's structurally absent here. Big win.
- Nested-ISR preemption is just another interleaving the protocol tolerates: a higher-priority producing ISR preempts a lower one mid-fill → it claims a *different* slot (MPSC `producer_idx` already advanced), commits; the consumer waits in-order at the lower slot until the preempted ISR resumes and commits. No corruption.

**N producing ISRs → 1 consuming ISR** — a single shared MPSC ring:
- Producers CAS-claim, fill, commit — with `on_full = |_| false` so a full ring makes the ISR **bail**, never spin.
- The consuming ISR drains with `on_empty = |_| false`, bailing when empty.
- The tombstone/unwind path is irrelevant — ISRs run panic=abort, so a fill never unwinds.

**N:M (fan-out / consolidation)** — there is no N:M *ring*; it's a **graph**, which is exactly the topology we just documented:
- Consolidate (N→1): shared MPSC ring, or fan-in of N SPSC rings.
- Fan-out (1→M): one producer, M ring edges (the fan-out case we just added).
- N:M: wire the two — N producers into a consolidation node, then fan out to M consumers, or a routing node in between. All nodes can be ISRs.

**The constraints that come with "all-ISR"** (these are the design teeth):
1. **Non-blocking only** — ISRs must use bail-immediately policies. No `spin`. So backpressure can't be "wait" — it must be **drop, or overflow-FIFO** (todo #2). This makes the overflow work *more* central for an ISR system than a thread one.
2. **Doorbell / trigger** — a consuming ISR only runs when *something fires it*. In an all-ISR system a producer typically has to raise a software interrupt / IPI to run the consumer after committing. The ring doesn't provide that; it's a system-level trigger (the header's user-line "blocking contract" is the natural seam, but for ISRs it's a soft-IRQ trigger, not a futex).
3. **Atomics floor splits the two styles** — the shared MPSC ring needs CAS (`target_has_atomic = "32"`, ≥ Cortex-M3). On a **Cortex-M0 / thumbv6m (no CAS)**, N producers can't share an MPSC ring — you *must* use fan-in (N SPSC rings, load/store only). This is a concrete reason both many-to-one styles have to exist, now with a hard target-driven answer.
4. **CAS retry latency** under heavy nested-ISR contention is bounded but not constant — usually fine, worth noting for hard-real-time budgets.

So: possible, and it lands cleanly on primitives you already have — the new part is naming the *system-level* pieces (non-blocking backpressure, the doorbell, the CAS-vs-fan-in target split).

## Recording it as a design goal

This is a real new goal, not a wording tweak, so I don't want to just drop it into `## Goal` unilaterally. My suggestion:

- Add a line to **`## Goal`** naming ISR-to-ISR (all-ISR) messaging as an explicit target,
- and a **`### ISR-only systems`** subsection (near Execution contexts, or under it) capturing the four constraints above + the N:1 / N:M composition, with a todo pointer for the doorbell/backpressure pieces that aren't built yet.

Before I write: is that the right home and scope? And do you want the doorbell/trigger + non-blocking-backpressure treated as *in-scope for this goal* (so they become todo items), or just noted as prerequisites? Tell me the framing and I'll draft it.

1	# Ring buffer design
2	
3	Design notes for the zero-copy ring buffer. This file uses
4	[Prose form](../AGENTS.md#prose-form). It covers terminology,
5	requirements, constraints, the memory layout, the API, and
6	the validation ladder, and is kept in sync with the
7	implementation in `src/` (as-built): `lib.rs` holds the
8	region-level machinery (header, init/attach/split, geometry
9	helpers) and re-exports the endpoint modules `producer.rs`
10	(Producer, WriteSlot, Full) and `consumer.rs` (Consumer,
11	ReadSlot, Empty).
12	
13	## Goal
14	
15	A ring buffer for passing typed messages between a producer and
16	a consumer with no copying at the boundary: the producer writes
17	its message directly into buffer memory, the consumer reads it
18	in place. The same layout must work intra-process (threads) and
19	inter-process (shared memory).
20	
21	## Terminology
22	
23	The vocabulary the rest of the document builds on, defined
24	once before first use:
25	
26	- **slot** — a cache-line-aligned `[u8; N]`: one of the
27	  ring's M fixed-size buffers. Untyped storage — a message
28	  is a zerocopy `&T` / `&mut T` view into it (geometry
29	  details in [Constraints](#constraints)).
30	- **reserve** — take exclusive access to a slot through a
31	  guard:
32	  - the producer reserves the next free slot
33	    (`WriteSlot`);
34	  - the consumer reserves the oldest unread slot
35	    (`ReadSlot`).
36	- **commit** — the producer publishes its slot to the
37	  consumer; the handoff of ownership away from the writer:
38	  - SPSC: the `producer_idx` Release store;
39	  - MPSC: the slot's seq Release store.
40	- **release** — the consumer hands its slot back for
41	  reuse; the matching handoff in the other direction:
42	  - SPSC: the `consumer_idx` Release store;
43	  - MPSC: the slot's seq Release store (`pos + M`).
44	- **claim** — MPSC only: a producer wins exclusive
45	  ownership of a slot position by CAS on the shared
46	  producer index; writing and committing follow. Claim
47	  order and commit order can differ — see
48	  [MPSC ring (sibling primitive)](#mpsc-ring-sibling-primitive).
49	
50	## Requirements
```

The same layout must work intra-process (threads) and\ninter-process (shared memory).\n\n## Terminology",

The same layout must work intra-process (threads) and\ninter-process (shared memory).\n\n**Blue-sky goal: ISR-to-ISR messaging.** A stretch target —\nthe same rings carrying messages between *interrupt service\nroutines*, no thread in the loop: N producing ISRs into one\nconsuming ISR, up to N:M for fan-out / consolidation (entities\nas a graph of ISR nodes,\n[Execution contexts](#execution-contexts)). Lock-free rings\nsuit ISRs — no lock means no priority inversion — but the\ngotchas are real:\n\n- **No blocking, ever** — an ISR bails, never waits, on Full\n  or Empty (the `|_| false` policy); backpressure must be drop\n  or an overflow FIFO, never a spin.\n- **Something must fire the consumer** — a consuming ISR runs\n  only when triggered, so an all-ISR system needs a doorbell\n  (a software interrupt / IPI raised after commit); the ring\n  does not provide it.\n- **The CAS floor splits the styles by target** — a shared\n  MPSC ring needs `target_has_atomic = \"32\"` (≥ Cortex-M3);\n  on a no-CAS M0 / thumbv6m, N producers must fan in over N\n  SPSC rings (load/store only) instead.\n- **Latency is bounded, not constant** — MPSC CAS retries\n  under nested-ISR contention are bounded, but a hard\n  real-time budget must account for them.\n- **panic=abort assumed** — the tombstone/unwind path never\n  runs in an ISR, so a panicking fill aborts rather than\n  degrading; fine, but a property to design around.\n\n## Terminology"

> AGENT

Done — the blue-sky goal is in `## Goal`, clearly marked aspirational, with the five gotchas as sub-bullets and a link to Execution contexts.

On the **BNO085** — one honest caveat so we don't bake a wrong assumption into the design later. As I understand it, the M0+ inside the BNO085 is the sensor's *internal* hub running Hillcrest/CEVA's closed SH-2 fusion firmware; it's not user-programmable — you talk to it over I2C/SPI/UART (SHTP frames). So "our message system running on *it*" probably isn't on the BNO085's own core. But your instinct is still dead-on: M0+ is precisely the no-CAS **thumbv6m** target that exercises the fan-in path, so a separate M0/M0+ host (RP2040, SAMD21, an M0+ dev board) talking to the BNO085 would be a real validation of the "N producers must fan in, no shared MPSC" branch. Worth chasing.

I deliberately kept the BNO085 *out* of the design doc — it's a specific, still-uncertain hardware bet, not a design fact. If you want to track it, the natural home is a `## Ideas` entry in `notes/todo.md` ("validate thumbv6m fan-in path on an M0+ host") — say the word and I'll add it.

# Adafruit 9-DOF Orientation IMU Fusion Breakout - BNO085

**Product Name:** Adafruit 9-DOF Orientation IMU Fusion Breakout - BNO085 (BNO080) - STEMMA QT / Qwiic

**Main Chip:** BNO085 inertial measurement unit (IMU) with an embedded Arm Cortex M0 processor

**Type:** This is not a user-programmable microcontroller board. Rather, it's a sensor breakout board designed to interface with external microcontrollers via I2C, SPI, or UART.

**Key Specifications:**
- 9 degrees of freedom: 3-axis accelerometer, gyroscope, and magnetometer
- Dimensions: 25.6mm × 22.7mm × 4.6mm
- Includes onboard voltage regulator and level-shifting circuitry for 3.3V or 5V logic
- Two STEMMA QT connectors for solderless connections
- 32.768kHz crystal reference oscillator
- Price: $24.95

**Core Capabilities:**
The sensor performs on-board fusion calculations, delivering orientation quaternions, calibrated sensor vectors, and specialized detection outputs (stability, tap, step, shake detection, and activity classification) without requiring external processing.
```

> AGENT

That's the same sensor — Adafruit 4754 is the **BNO085 9-DOF breakout** (BNO085/BNO080, STEMMA QT). The "Arm Cortex-M0" on it is exactly the internal fusion hub I flagged: closed SH-2 firmware, **not user-programmable**. You talk to it over I2C/SPI/UART; you can't load our message system onto its M0. So as a *host* target, this one's a no-go — it's a sensor, not a board.

But it pairs perfectly with a real M0+ host, and those are what you actually want for the thumbv6m/fan-in validation:

- **RP2040 boards** — Adafruit Feather RP2040, QT Py RP2040. Dual **Cortex-M0+** (ARMv6-M): **no LDREX/STREX → no native CAS**, Rust target `thumbv6m-none-eabi`. That's precisely the "no shared MPSC, must fan in" branch — *and* it's dual-core, so you can exercise true cross-core SPSC too.
- **SAMD21 boards** — Feather M0, QT Py SAMD21, Trinket M0. Single Cortex-M0+, same no-CAS thumbv6m story.

Both have STEMMA QT, so this exact BNO085 breakout plugs straight in. And it gives you a genuinely on-point test of the blue-sky goal: the BNO085's **data-ready interrupt** fires an ISR on the M0+ host, that ISR reads the sample over I2C and **produces into a ring**, a consumer drains it — an ISR-producer on a no-CAS target, validating the fan-in path end-to-end.

> AGENT

Yes — the ESP32 is the one thing in that setup you *can* program (unlike the BNO085's locked M0). But it validates a *different* branch than the M0 does, so it's worth being precise about what it buys you:

**What the ESP32 is:** dual-core **Xtensa LX6** (not ARM). Crucially, it **has** atomic compare-swap (the `S32C1I` instruction), so `target_has_atomic = "32"` is true — it runs the **shared MPSC ring** natively. Programmable in Rust via esp-rs / `esp-hal` (no_std); the Xtensa core needs the `espup` toolchain (the RISC-V ESP32 variants — C3/C6 — use upstream Rust instead).

**What it validates (a lot):**
- ✅ The message system running on a real MCU, no_std.
- ✅ **Cross-core** SPSC/MPSC — it's genuinely dual-core, so two cores hand off through a ring, not just preemptive threads.
- ✅ **ISR-producer** — the BNO085's INT pin → an ESP32 GPIO ISR → read the sample over I2C → produce into a ring. That's a direct chunk of the blue-sky ISR goal, on real hardware.

**What it does *not* validate:**
- ❌ The **no-CAS fan-in branch**. The ESP32 *has* atomics, so it takes the MPSC path — the same path a desktop takes. The whole reason the M0 was interesting is that it *lacks* CAS and forces fan-in. The ESP32 skips that fork entirely.

So they're complementary: **ESP32 = fastest path to "does it run, cross-core, ISR-producer" on hardware**; **M0+ (RP2040/SAMD21) = the specific proof that the fan-in fallback works where CAS doesn't exist.** The BNO085 breakout plugs into either over I2C.

That keeps the has-CAS and no-CAS validation paths recorded without touching the design doc.

Back to our docs.
"**ISR, sole ower of an endpoint**" Can the ISR code be the consumer and multiple threads can be the producer and our current MPSC works without interrupt disable but if we use EI/DI then an ISR can be the consumer and multiple ISRs and threads could be producers?

> AGENT

Great question — and the answer splits cleanly, but not along the axis you'd expect. The thing that decides whether you need EI/DI is **whether the hardware has CAS**, *not* whether a producer is an ISR.

## Q1: ISR consumer + N thread producers — yes, no EI/DI

The current MPSC handles this as-is. The consumer is the **sole owner of the consumer endpoint**, and it works because:

- The consumer **never touches `producer_idx`** — it reads `seq[c]` (atomic, Acquire), reads the slot, then releases `seq[c] = c+M` and advances its **private** `consumer_idx`.
- Every shared word is single-writer + atomic: `consumer_idx` (consumer only), `producer_idx` (producers only, via CAS), `seq[i]` (producer-commit then consumer-release, phase-separated, never concurrent on one slot).
- An ISR consumer firing mid-way through a producer thread's claim only ever drains **committed** slots; the slot the producer is still filling has `seq != c+1`, so the in-order consumer stops there. Disjoint. No interrupt disable.

This is exactly your "ISR, sole owner of an endpoint" bullet — just with the ISR on the *consumer* side and MPSC on the producer side.

## Q2: add ISR producers — EI/DI only matters on no-CAS hardware

Here's the reframe. What multiple producers need is an **atomic claim** (the RMW on `producer_idx`). Who provides that atomicity depends on the target:

- **Has-CAS target (Cortex-M3+, ESP32, desktop):** you do **not** need EI/DI, even to add ISR producers. The current MPSC already supports **N producers (threads *and* ISRs) → 1 consumer (thread *or* ISR)** with no interrupt disable. The CAS is LL/SC (LDREX/STREX): if an ISR preempts a producer mid-claim, the STREX fails and it retries; if the producer already won and is filling, the ISR claims a *different* slot. ISRs run to completion, so they commit promptly. It just works.
- **No-CAS target (Cortex-M0 / thumbv6m):** there's no hardware CAS for the claim. **This** is where EI/DI earns its keep — disable interrupts around the `producer_idx` read-modify-write to emulate the atomic claim, and then yes, ISRs + threads can all be producers on the shared MPSC ring.

So your instinct is right, but the trigger is the missing CAS, not the ISR. EI/DI is a **substitute for a CAS the M0 doesn't have** — it's the same thing hardware CAS does on bigger cores.

Two caveats on the M0 EI/DI path:
- It **lengthens interrupt latency** — the claim blocks *all* interrupts (including the consumer ISR) for those few instructions. Keep the critical section tiny.
- It's **single-core only**. On a dual-core no-CAS part (RP2040), disabling interrupts on one core doesn't stop the other — you'd also need a hardware spinlock (the RP2040's SIO locks, which is how `portable-atomic` emulates CAS there).

And the alternative to EI/DI on M0 is the one already in the doc: **fan-in** — give each producer its own SPSC ring (no shared claim, no CAS, no EI/DI at all). So on no-CAS hardware the real design fork is **EI/DI-emulated shared MPSC** vs **fan-in of SPSC rings**.

That last fork (EI/DI-emulated CAS as a *third* option beside "shared MPSC" and "fan-in") isn't in the doc yet — the blue-sky gotcha currently jumps straight from "no CAS" to "fan-in." Want me to add EI/DI-emulated-claim as the middle option there, or keep it in conversation until you've actually tried it on the M0+?

> AGENT

Done. The gotcha now lays out the full fork:

> - **The CAS floor splits the styles by target** — a shared MPSC ring's multi-producer claim needs an atomic RMW on `producer_idx`:
>   - has-CAS (`target_has_atomic = "32"`, ≥ Cortex-M3) — hardware CAS provides the claim, nothing extra needed.
>   - no-CAS (M0 / thumbv6m) — two ways to keep N producers:
>     - EI/DI-emulated claim: disable interrupts around the RMW to stand in for the CAS. Keeps the shared ring. Costs a brief all-interrupt blackout per claim, and is single-core only — a second core still needs a hardware spinlock.
>     - fan-in: each producer gets its own SPSC ring — no shared claim, no CAS, no EI/DI — and the consumer polls the N rings.

So the no-CAS branch now presents EI/DI-emulated shared MPSC and fan-in as the two real choices, with the EI/DI cost (interrupt-latency blackout, single-core-only) called out.

1	# Cycle protocol
2	
3	This protocol uses [Prose form](../AGENTS.md#prose-form). It
4	contains instructions on how a commit cycle is accomplished.
5	
6	The artifact a cycle produces is whatever the bot generates from
7	the conversation — code, prose, an image, a song, a screenplay.
8	The steps below use a Rust crate as the running example (the
9	cargo cycle, `Cargo.toml` versioning); substitute your medium's
10	equivalents — this project's manifest is recorded in
11	[versioning.md](versioning.md).
12	
13	## Cycles
14	
15	A cycle has three phases:
16	
17	- **[Preparation](#preparation)** (`X.Y.Z-0`) — the cycle's
18	  first commit, when it needs setup (a lightweight cycle omits
19	  it and starts at `-1` — see
20	  [versioning.md](versioning.md#step-numbering)). Sets up the
21	  cycle:
22	  - Bump the version-of-record to `X.Y.Z-0` (where it lives
23	    and the suffix scheme are project-specific — see
24	    [versioning.md](versioning.md)).
25	  - Pick up a `## Todo` item (typically the top-ranked,
26	    #1) into `## In Progress` (bold title + succinct problem
27	    statement + plan ladder).
28	  - Open the [chores section](#chores-sections).
29	- **[Work-N](#work-n)** (`X.Y.Z-1`, `X.Y.Z-2`, …) — the
30	  commits that implement the change. As many as the change
31	  needs; each runs through the
32	  [per-commit flow](#per-commit-flow).
33	- **[Close-out](#close-out)** (bare `X.Y.Z`) — the cycle's
34	  last commit. Bookkeeping only:
35	  - Move the picked-up item to a one-line entry in
36	    `## Done`.
37	  - Move the `## In Progress` block into the
38	    [chores section](#chores-sections).
39	  - Optionally update `notes/README.md` if functionality
40	    changed.
41	
42	A cycle's commits are published to the project remote
43	either incrementally or as one batch at close-out; the
44	result must always be published at close-out. See
45	[Pushing](#pushing).
46	
47	**Sub-cycles.** When a Work commit's scope grows enough to
48	warrant its own ladder, it subdivides — `X.Y.Z-3.0`
49	Preparation, `X.Y.Z-3.1` / `X.Y.Z-3.2` Work, `X.Y.Z-3`
50	Close-out. The same three-phase shape applies recursively
51	at every depth. See [Numbering](#numbering) for the
52	suffix rule and [Sub-cycle ladders](#sub-cycle-ladders)
53	for the local-ladder mechanics.
54	
55	## Chores sections
56	
57	A **chores section** is a `##` section in
58	`notes/chores/chores-NN.md` recording landed work. In
59	general, every commit that lands on the permanent branch
60	should have a reference to it on a `Commits:` list in a
61	chores file.
62	
63	The phrase **"Open" the chores section** means append a
64	`##` header to the current `notes/chores/chores-NN.md`
65	with the title it records (e.g. `## refactor: foo bar`),
66	followed by an **empty `Commits:`** line. The narrative
67	body stays empty until close-out (see [Close-out](#close-out));
68	the `Commits:` line is backfilled later, once the commit is
69	permanent (see [Commits backfill](#commits-backfill) below).
70	
71	Fuller chores conventions (content rules, header sync,
72	design subsection pattern, `Commits:` formatting) live in
73	AGENTS.md [Chores conventions](../AGENTS.md#chores-conventions).
74	
75	### Commits backfill
76	
77	A chores section's `Commits:` line cites the commit(s) it
78	records, by SHA — but a SHA isn't stable until the commit
79	lands on a **permanent branch** (`main`, or a long-lived
80	release/patch branch that won't be rewritten); a rebase or
81	squash rewrites it on the way. So:
82	
83	- A chores section is **opened with an empty `Commits:`** line.
84	- **Backfill once the commit is on a permanent branch**,
85	  where its SHA is final. A commit can't record its own SHA
86	  (that would change the hash), so the fill always lands one
87	  push later: **each push backfills the `Commits:` of the
88	  commits the previous push made permanent.** On a topic
89	  branch the sections instead wait until the branch lands —
90	  so no SHA is ever written that a later rebase could
91	  invalidate.
92	
93	Use `[[N]]` refs — several as `[[N]],[[M]]` only when one
94	section records multiple commits (a merge non-ff close-out) —
95	with the commit URL + 40-hex SHA in the file's `# References`
96	(format in AGENTS.md
97	[Chores commit references](../AGENTS.md#chores-commit-references)).
98	A section's `##` title matches its commit title, so a rare
99	deliberate rewrite of a permanent-branch commit re-syncs via
100	`git log --grep "<title>"`.
101	
102	The per-push cadence is a project choice, not dogma — a
103	**per-close-out** model (recording a cycle's SHAs at its
104	close-out) is equally valid. The one invariant: a recorded
105	SHA must be permanent.
106	
107	## Preparation
108	
109	The cycle's first commit (`X.Y.Z-0`), when the cycle needs
110	setup (a lightweight cycle omits it — see
111	[versioning.md](versioning.md#step-numbering)):
112	
113	- **Bump the version-of-record** to `X.Y.Z-0`. Where it
114	  lives, the suffix scheme, and any derived files (a
115	  lockfile, a sourced manifest version) are
116	  project-specific — see [versioning.md](versioning.md).
117	- **Move a `## Todo` item** (if the cycle has one) into
118	  `## In Progress` and the todo item should have:
119	  - A **bold title line** — that will be the chores
120	    section header, minus the `## ` prefix.
121	  - A **succinct problem statement**; add if one is needed
122	  - A **plan ladder**.
123	- **Open the [chores section](#chores-sections)** —
124	  append a `##` header with the title it records — the
125	  cycle's anticipated close-out title.
126	
127	## Work-N
128	
129	The cycle's work commits (`X.Y.Z-1`, `X.Y.Z-2`, …)
130	implement the change. As many as needed:
131	
132	- Each commit runs through the
133	  **[per-commit flow](#per-commit-flow)**.
134	- **Interim pushes** are optional (backup, progress
135	  visibility).
136	- Close-out is the only mandatory push (see
137	  [Pushing](#pushing)).
138	- **Subdivide into a sub-cycle** if a Work commit's
139	  scope grows enough (see
140	  [Sub-cycle ladders](#sub-cycle-ladders)).
141	
142	## Close-out
143	
144	The cycle's last commit (bare `X.Y.Z`) does bookkeeping
145	only — the commit body describes that bookkeeping, not
146	what happens post-squash:
147	
148	- **Move the picked-up item** from `## In Progress` to a
149	  one-line entry (with a chores `[N]` ref) in `## Done`.
150	- **Move the `## In Progress` block into the
151	  [chores section](#chores-sections)** (the title-only
152	  placeholder opened during Preparation):
153	  - Cut the problem statement + plan ladder out of
154	    `## In Progress` (drop the bold title line — the
155	    chores `##` header already carries that string).
156	  - Paste under the chores `##` header.
157	  - Sync the chores header to the **final** commit title
158	    if the cycle's scope shifted; update every anchor
159	    back-reference.
160	  - Add an `### As-built ladder` listing the cycle's
161	    commits; optional `### Outcome` notes.
162	  - Replace `## In Progress` with
163	    `_No cycle currently in progress._`.
164	- **Update `notes/README.md`** if functionality changed
165	  (new flags, new subcommands, changed behavior).
166	
167	Whether to **squash** the cycle into one commit before the
168	publishing push, or push as-is, is decided at push time —
169	see [Pushing](#pushing).
170	
171	## Numbering
172	
173	Each commit's phase is encoded in the version suffix — `-0`
174	Preparation, `-1`/`-2`/… Work, bare `X.Y.Z` Close-out,
175	recursively for sub-cycles. The full scheme — disambiguation,
176	nesting, optional Preparation, the project's version-of-record
177	format, and the per-phase bump — lives in
178	[versioning.md](versioning.md#step-numbering), which is the
179	single source of truth for this repo's versioning.
180	
181	## Per-commit flow
182	
183	Every commit (Preparation, each Work commit, Close-out) goes
184	through:
185	
186	1. **Mark this commit `(current)`** as the first edit in
187	   `notes/todo.md > ## In Progress`.
188	2. **Do the work** (see [Iterative work](#iterative-work)
189	   for the loop-and-squash technique).
190	3. **Flip this commit `(current)` → `(done)`** in `## In
191	   Progress` — before the cargo cycle and the commit.
192	4. **Validate the artifact** — a medium-specific step, skip-able
193	   for notes-only commits, mandatory at close-out. For the Rust
194	   example the cargo cycle is:
195	   1. `cargo fmt`
196	   2. `cargo clippy --all-targets -- -D warnings`
197	   3. `cargo test`
198	   4. `cargo install --path . --locked`
199	   5. (re-test if anything substantive changed)
200	5. **Work review.** Stop *before* writing any description;
201	   tell the user "ready to commit." The user reviews the
202	   changes and we iterate until complete.
203	6. **Write the commit description** — see
204	   [Commit description](#commit-description).
205	7. **Commit Description review.** Show the title + body
206	   and stop. The user reviews the description. Iterate.
207	8. **Commit.** `jj commit -m "title" -m "body" -R .` for
208	   the app repo, `-R .claude` for the bot repo (`-R` last
209	   keeps the verb visible):
210	
211	   ```
212	   jj commit -m \
213	   "<type>: <short description>" \
214	   -m "<intro paragraph>
215	
216	   - file1: gist
217	   - file2: gist
218	
219	   ochid: /.claude/<chid>" \
220	   -R .
221	   ```
222	
223	**Two overrides apply:**
224	
225	- **Deviation or question** — any time the work deviates
226	  from the agreed plan, or a question arises, stop and
227	  surface it; don't push through.
228	- **ESC-ESC** — the user can interrupt at any point to pull
229	  a review or question forward.
230	
231	## Commit description
232	
233	[Conventional Commits](https://www.conventionalcommits.org/):
234	
235	```
236	<type>: <short description>
237	```
238	
239	Titles carry **no trailing `(<version>)` suffix**. The
240	version-of-record — where it lives and its bump cadence, see
241	[versioning.md](versioning.md) — is useful for confirming
242	you're running the version you're testing, not a per-commit
243	title marker. We think per-commit version-in-title is
244	unstable on large projects: many changes are in flight
245	simultaneously, and a
246	version is only stable once merged into the main repo — so
247	titles don't carry one.
248	
249	### Title
250	
251	- ≤50 chars total.
252	- Common types: `feat`, `fix`, `refactor`, `test`,
253	  `docs`, `chore`.
254	- Favor terse phrasings.
255	- **Distinct per step** — each of a cycle's commits gets its
256	  own descriptive title (no shared cycle title with a step
257	  marker). Share a greppable stem across the cycle's titles
258	  (e.g. `ring buffer`) so `git log --grep` collects them; the
259	  chores section header matches the close-out title. See
260	  AGENTS.md
261	  [Conventional-commit shape](../AGENTS.md#conventional-commit-shape-ladder--chores--commit).
262	
263	### Body
264	
265	[Prose form](../AGENTS.md#prose-form) (intro + bullets),
266	wrap ≤72. Bullet content differs per repo:
267	
268	- **App-repo body**: file-by-file. One bullet per file
269	  changed (file plus a one-line gist). Sub-bullets for
270	  files with multiple distinct changes:
271	
272	  ```
273	  - path/to/file1
274	    - first distinct change
275	    - second distinct change, wrapping to next line
276	       with continuation indented 5
277	  ```
278	
279	  The file-by-file list is the source of truth for the
280	  cycle's mechanical change record. Chores carries the
281	  narrative + design, not a copy of it. Promote any
282	  "why" beyond one sentence to a chores `###` subsection.
283	
284	- **Session-repo (`.claude`) body**: bullets describe
285	  in-session activity rather than code changes.
286	
287	### Trailer
288	
289	`ochid:` as the last line of the body — see
290	[Cross-repo linking (ochid trailers)](../AGENTS.md#cross-repo-linking-ochid-trailers)
291	in AGENTS.md for the convention.
292	
293	For breaking changes, use the hyphenated `BREAKING-CHANGE:`
294	trailer key. `BREAKING CHANGE:` (with a space) is the only
295	space-separated key the Conventional Commits spec allows; the
296	hyphenated form is also valid and avoids the space ambiguity.
297	
298	## Reviewing changes
299	
300	Work review looks at the **uncommitted working-copy diff**,
301	on the way to commit. The user opens diffs in their
302	editor (Zed, VSCode); jj commands are for terminal:
303	
304	- `jj diff` — working-copy diff (uncommitted)
305	- `jj diff -r @-` — diff of the previous commit
306	- `jj diff --from <X> --to <Y>` — any two revisions
307	- `jj show -r <X>` — description + diff for one rev
308	
309	Don't `jj edit -r @-` to view a past commit — that marks
310	it mutable and shifts `@`; use `jj diff -r @-` or
311	`jj show -r @-`.
312	
313	See [Sub-cycle ladders](#sub-cycle-ladders) for the
314	close-out squash recipe and recovery; revset primitives
315	are in [`jj-tips.md`](jj-tips.md#revsets).
316	
317	## Pushing
318	
319	### Policy
320	
321	Push is **discretionary** during the cycle (backup,
322	progress visibility) and **mandatory at close-out** —
323	the cycle's result must be published.
324	
325	**Approval is per-push.** Every push — any repo, any kind:
326	cycle push, interim backup, recovery/surgery force-push —
327	happens only after the user has reviewed the changes to be
328	published and explicitly approved that specific push.
329	Approval of a plan that *includes* a push does not authorize
330	the push itself; stop and ask again at the moment of pushing.
331	
332	**Default is interactive; an explicit scoped delegation waives
333	the gates.** The gates above — per-push approval, the
334	commit-description review (show title+body and stop), and the
335	hard stop after push/finalize — are the *interactive default*.
336	They yield when the user **explicitly** delegates a complete,
337	bounded task and authorizes carrying it through ("do all of X
338	and push each step, don't check in"). The bot then proceeds
339	through that task's commits and pushes without stopping, and
340	continues past each push to the next step. Conditions:
341	
342	- **Explicit grant** — never inferred from a task merely being
343	  well-scoped; the user's words must authorize unattended
344	  completion. "Commit and push" (or "then push") names the
345	  destination, not a waiver: it authorizes the push *after*
346	  the normal work review and description review, not skipping
347	  them. Only wording that explicitly waives the stops ("don't
348	  check in", "no need to review", "carry it through
349	  unattended") waives them.
350	- **Bounded goal** — covers the named task only; does not carry
351	  to the next task or a vaguer follow-on.
352	- **Destructive ops still pause** — delegation covers the task's
353	  ordinary commits and pushes; it does *not* pre-authorize a
354	  genuinely irreversible action (force-push over published
355	  history, history rewrite, deleting a remote branch). Those can
356	  permanently destroy work and aren't a normal cycle step, so the
357	  bot flags one before acting. An ordinary delegated cycle never
358	  reaches this.
359	- **Still transparent** — report each commit/push as it lands
360	  (title + outcome) so the user can catch up.
361	- **When in doubt, ask** — ambiguous authorization falls back to
362	  per-push approval.
363	
364	### Shape at close-out push
365	
366	At close-out the cycle's *work* is done; its *published
367	shape* is the remaining choice, made at push time. Surface
368	the options and get user approval before pushing — once on
369	the target, changing shape is a remote rewrite (force-push,
370	needs approval), so choose deliberately.
371	
372	- **Squash to one commit** — single entry on the target.
373	  Right for straightforward changes where the Work-N is
374	  focused on one or two files.
375	- **Merge non-ff** *(current default)* — `main` gains a
376	  merge commit (`X.Y.Z`); cycle commits stay reachable via
377	  two parents. `jj log -r ..@ -n <N>` shows the trapezoidal
378	  shape. See [Merge non-ff recipe](#merge-non-ff-recipe)
379	  for the full setup sequence.
380	- **Keep separate** — one commit per cycle entry on
381	  `main`. Use when the decomposition itself is
382	  informative. Each chores section keeps its own header /
383	  `Commits:` ref; no consolidation churn.
384	
385	Set up squash/merge before invoking `vc-x1 push`; use
386	`jj git push` directly for non-standard shapes.
387	
388	### Merge non-ff recipe
389	
390	Setting up a [Merge non-ff](#shape-at-close-out-push)
391	close-out is a fixed sequence. `<closeout>` is the cycle's
392	close-out commit, `<prev>` the previous cycle's close-out
393	(the current `main` tip), `<work-tip>` the cycle's last
394	Work commit:
395	
396	1. **Rebase the close-out into a merge** — `jj rebase -r
397	   <closeout> --onto <prev> --onto <work-tip>`.
398	   - `-r <closeout>` keeps the `<closeout>` commit in place.
399	   - `--onto <prev>` becomes its first parent (trunk).
400	   - `--onto <work-tip>` becomes the second parent.
401	
402	   Together these make `<closeout>` a merge of
403	   `<prev>` + `<work-tip>`, forming a trapezoidal commit.
404	2. **Use `jj new <merge>`** to add an empty `@` above the
405	   merge. The rebase left `@` *on* the now-content-bearing
406	   merge, which git/IDE diff views show as uncommitted;
407	   `jj new` restores the clean empty `@` on top.
408	3. **Push** — `jj git push --bookmark main -R .`.
409	
410	**Post-hoc caveat.** If the cycle was already pushed
411	[Keep separate](#shape-at-close-out-push) its commits are
412	immutable: the rebase needs `--ignore-immutable` and the
413	push force-updates `main`.
414	The standard sequence assumes the merge is set up *before*
415	the close-out push.
416	
417	### vc-x1 push wrapper
418	
419	`vc-x1 push <bookmark>` wraps per-push mechanics. See
420	`vc-x1 push --help` for current flags.
421	
422	**Current limitation**: only fully supports the
423	[Keep separate](#shape-at-close-out-push) shape; other
424	shapes need manual jj steps. Improvements tracked /
425	planned:
426	
427	- N:1 code↔bot for Merge non-ff (`## Todo` entry P1).
428	- Symmetric squash (planned, to be captured in `-2`).
429	- Per-repo bookmark names (planned).
430	
431	### .claude cadence
432	
433	**Cadence**: one push = one `.claude` commit, paired
434	with every code commit in that push.
435	
436	The `.claude` working copy accumulates session data
437	across the cycle; its change ID stays stable across
438	snapshots, `jj describe`, and the finalize commit, so
439	code-side `ochid:` trailers resolve.
440	
441	`.claude` is a linear journal — all session work lives
442	on `main`, regardless of the app-side bookmark. **Do not
443	create or maintain `.claude` bookmarks that mirror
444	app-side branches** — risks the bot steering session
445	pushes to the wrong remote ref.
446	
447	Ending a session: if the user runs `/exit` there will be
448	session information created, which we don't worry about.
449	The user can close the terminal instead and `@` will
450	remain empty.
451	
452	### Bot communication at the reviews
453	
454	Use plain prose — no insider jargon ("Gate N signal",
455	"Checkpoint N", etc.):
456	
457	- **At Work review** — summarize what changed and stop.
458	  "Work complete. Please review."
459	- **At Commit Description review** — present `$TITLE`
460	  and `$BODY` explicitly; ask permission to commit/push.
461	  Don't spell out the full `vc-x1 push ... --title ...
462	  --body ...` invocation by default.
463	- **At Post close-out review** — surface the shape
464	  options (squash / merge / keep) and the push target;
465	  wait for the user's choice before any `jj squash` /
466	  `jj rebase` / `jj git push` invocation.
467	
468	### After push or finalize: stop and wait
469	
470	After you **push** (cross the remote boundary) or launch
471	**finalize** on the `.claude` repo (`vc-x1 finalize --repo
472	.claude --squash --push <bookmark> --delay 10 --detach`) — by
473	hand or via the `vc-x1 push` wrapper — you **MUST NEVER**
474	proceed (next step, edit, tool call, text output) until the
475	user explicitly directs you to continue. **Even when the next
476	step seems obvious — wait.** Treat push-or-finalize as a hard
477	stop for the whole turn.
478	
479	`vc-x1 push` performs both as its tail stages — `push-app`
480	then the detached `vc-x1 finalize` on `.claude` — so there is
481	no gap left to speak in once the push is invoked. Put **all**
482	closing words *before* invoking it. The harness rejects an empty
483	turn, so it may force a visible token after the tool returns; if
484	so, emit a bare acknowledgment only (e.g. "landed") — never a
485	summary, verification, or next-step offer.
486	
487	Why (`.claude` is the live journal finalize snapshots →
488	squashes → pushes):
489	
490	- **`--detach`**: lets the tool return immediately so the
491	  harness can keep flushing the *trailing* session-data — the
492	  tool call's own transcript and any words emitted *before*
493	  the invoke — into the snapshot.
494	- **`--delay 10`**: gives the harness time to complete that
495	  flush before the snapshot is taken. It is **not** time for
496	  the bot to do more work.
497	- **silence**: the bot must not summarize or perform any work
498	  after the tool returns. The harness may force a visible token
499	  (it rejects an empty turn) — keep it to a bare acknowledgment,
500	  never a summary or a next step.
501	
502	**Known slip**: the bot has emitted a summary after launching
503	finalize. There is no "harmless" closing line — if it's worth
504	saying, say it *before* the invoke.
505	
506	### Recovery
507	
508	- **If push exits before `finalize-claude`** (e.g.
509	  failure between `push-app` and `finalize-claude`), run
510	  finalize by hand:
511	
512	  ```
513	  vc-x1 finalize --repo .claude --squash --push <bookmark> --delay 10 --detach --log /tmp/vc-x1-finalize.log
514	  ```
515	
516	- **Run finalize again if `@` is non-empty** after the
517	  finalize's squash-and-commit (also desirable after
518	  extra activity by the bot's agents).
519	  - Why: finalize snapshots `.claude` at `--delay`, but the
520	    bot keeps writing session data while it runs — the tool's
521	    own invocation plus the bot's closing response land
522	    *after* the snapshot.
523	  - Safe to repeat: bot session data is append-only, so a
524	    re-run never conflicts or overwrites. (This could
525	    change; it is not under the user's control.)
526	  - Fix: run `vc-x1 finalize --repo .claude --squash --push
527	    <bookmark> --delay 10 --detach` another time to capture
528	    that tail; one extra pass is generally enough.
529	  - No guarantees: events outside the bot's control can leave
530	    `@` non-empty — e.g. the bot's back end may decide to
531	    squash/consolidate session data, which can take minutes
532	    and land after the snapshot. The remedy is the same: just
533	    run finalize again. This is why a single pass is never
534	    guaranteed to leave `@` empty.
535	- **Clear push's saved state** after any out-of-band
536	  recovery — `rm .vc-x1/push-state.toml` or `vc-x1 push
537	  <bookmark> --restart` — otherwise push resumes from a
538	  stale stage.
539	- **Late code-repo tweak after `push-app` succeeded**
540	  (e.g. updating AGENTS.md or memory) requires `jj
541	  squash --ignore-immutable` and a re-push; that is a
542	  remote rewrite and needs explicit approval like any
543	  push.
544	
545	## Iterative work
546	
547	When work for a single commit (the **target**) benefits
548	from incremental review, loop:
549	
550	1. `jj new -R .` — fresh empty `@` on top of the target.
551	2. Make the next round of changes.
552	3. User reviews the round (see
553	   [Reviewing changes](#reviewing-changes)).
554	4. `jj squash -R .` folds into the target and creates a
555	   new empty `@`.
556	5. If not done, go to step 2.
557	
558	Same jj mechanics as a
559	[sub-cycle ladder](#sub-cycle-ladders), but at
560	single-commit scope — the version
561	doesn't change.
562	
563	## Sub-cycle ladders
564	
565	When a Work commit subdivides into a sub-cycle (see
566	[Numbering](#numbering) for suffix nesting), its Work
567	commits typically live as a local jj `@` chain and
568	**collapse into the sub-cycle's Close-out** before the
569	parent cycle continues. Ladder commits are scratch —
570	for review and bisection only.
571	
572	### Per-Work-commit contract within a ladder
573	
574	For each Work commit in the ladder:
575	
576	1. `jj new -R .` — create a fresh empty `@`.
577	2. Do the commit's work.
578	3. Run the fast validation (Rust example: `cargo test
579	   --bins`). **Non-negotiable** — for code, build and clippy
580	   alone miss regressions until a later commit runs the full
581	   suite, raising bisection cost.
582	4. `jj describe -m "..." -m "..." -R .` — working title
583	   only; the sub-cycle Close-out collects everything
584	   into one final commit.
585	
586	### Navigating the ladder
587	
588	Common moves:
589	
590	- `jj log -r '<base>::' -R .` — see the whole ladder
591	  from its base.
592	- `jj edit -r <prefix> -R .` — jump `@` to any ladder
593	  commit by chid prefix; useful for bisection.
594	- `jj edit @-- -R .` — quick-jump back two commits.
595	- `jj diff -r <chid> -R .` — review one commit in
596	  isolation.
597	
598	Modifications to any ladder commit rewrite it in place;
599	descendants auto-rebase.
600	
601	### Close-out: squash the ladder
602	
603	When all ladder Work commits are done and tests pass:
604	
605	```
606	jj squash --from "<base>..@-" --into @ -u -R .
607	```
608	
609	`<base>` is the parent of the first ladder commit; `-u`
610	keeps `@`'s description and discards the sources'.
611	After squash, history is linear: `<base> → @`;
612	intermediate commits are auto-abandoned.
613	
614	Then `vc-x1 push <bookmark>` as for any other commit.
615	
616	For N = 1 the squash is a no-op (`<base>..@-` is empty
617	when `@-` is `<base>`); push the single commit directly.
618	
619	### Recovery
620	
621	If a ladder commit goes wrong, back out without losing
622	prior commits:
623	
624	- **Discard the current commit.** `jj abandon @ -R .`
625	  drops it; you get a fresh empty `@` on the same
626	  parent.
627	- **Edit an earlier commit.** `jj edit -r <chid> -R .`,
628	  make corrections, then `jj edit -r <last-ladder-chid>`
629	  to return. Descendants auto-rebase.
630	- **Discard the entire ladder.** `jj op log -R .` shows
631	  the op history; `jj op restore <op-id> -R .` reverts
632	  to that point. Full undo — removes *all* ladder work
633	  after the chosen op. Use only to start over.
634	
635	# References
636	
637	- [`jj-tips.md`](jj-tips.md#revsets) — revset primitives
638	  (chid/cid, `@`/`@-`/`@+`, `..`/`::` ranges, prefix matching).
639	- [`substep-test.sh`](substep-test.sh) — script that
640	  scaffolds a 4-revision ladder under `/tmp/substep-test`
641	  for squash-recipe experiments.
642	- The per-commit `cargo test --bins` gate exists because a
643	  regression introduced in an early ladder commit can go
644	  uncaught until a later commit runs the full suite, raising
645	  bisection cost.
646	
```

1	# Versioning
2	
3	How this project versions its commits and the running
4	artifact. The scheme — the cycle step-numbering and the
5	unique-per-commit aim — is generic and shared across projects:
6	this file is copied **verbatim**, with
7	[Recording the version-of-record](#recording-the-version-of-record)
8	covering each medium by conditional rather than per-project
9	edits.
10	
11	## Terms
12	
13	Three names, used as defined here across
14	[AGENTS.md](../AGENTS.md),
15	[cycle-protocol.md](cycle-protocol.md), and the notes files:
16	
17	- **version** — the per-commit version (e.g. `0.3.0-5.3.0`),
18	  written with `-` in prose, ladders, chores, todo, and commit
19	  talk; its suffix encodes the cycle phase (see
20	  [Step numbering](#step-numbering)).
21	- **version-of-record** — the authoritative stored copy of the
22	  version, in the project's manifest (see
23	  [Recording the version-of-record](#recording-the-version-of-record));
24	  a built or running artifact derives from it.
25	- **versioning** — the topic: this scheme as a whole.
26	
27	## Recording the version-of-record
28	
29	Where the version-of-record lives, how it's stored and
30	surfaced, and how often it changes — pick the case that fits
31	your medium:
32	
33	- **Manifest** — where the version-of-record is stored:
34	  - if Rust, `Cargo.toml` `[package].version`
35	  - if Python, `pyproject.toml` `[project].version` (or the
36	    committed config it's sourced from)
37	  - otherwise wherever the medium records it (a generic
38	    `version.toml`, a book's frontmatter, …) — add the case as
39	    needed
40	- **Notation** — how the `-` form is stored:
41	  - if the format allows `-` (TOML `version.toml`, `Cargo.toml`),
42	    store it verbatim
43	  - if it bars `-` (PEP 440's local segment, e.g. a Python
44	    project), remap to `+` — `0.3.0-5.3.0` → `0.3.0+5.3.0`: same
45	    version, just the stored spelling
46	- **Reporter** — how a built artifact surfaces the
47	  version-of-record:
48	  - if a CLI app, `<cli-app> -V`
49	  - if a TUI/GUI, add to Help/About or display on the title
50	- **Cadence** — how often to bump: see
51	  [Unique per commit](#unique-per-commit-preference-not-requirement);
52	  this project follows the per-commit preference.
53	
54	## Unique per commit (preference, not requirement)
55	
56	Our general notion is that the version-of-record should change
57	on **every commit**, so a built or running artifact identifies
58	the exact commit it came from.
59	
60	- This is a preference, **not** a hard requirement. A project
61	  following the cycle protocol may bump less often — once per
62	  cycle, only at release, and so on. Record the choice in
63	  [Recording the version-of-record](#recording-the-version-of-record)
64	  if it differs.
65	- It is achievable because the cycle's versions (below) are
66	  **pre-assignable**, unlike the git SHA, which a commit cannot
67	  contain (see the cycle protocol's
68	  [Commits backfill](cycle-protocol.md#commits-backfill)).
69	
70	## Step numbering
71	
72	The cycle (Preparation -> Work -> Close-out; see
73	[cycle-protocol.md](cycle-protocol.md)) encodes each commit's
74	phase in the version suffix — the **final identifier `0` marks
75	a Preparation**:
76	
77	- `X.Y.Z-0` — Preparation
78	- `X.Y.Z-1`, `X.Y.Z-2`, … — Work commits
79	- `X.Y.Z` — Close-out (bare version, no suffix)
80	
81	**Preparation is optional.** A lightweight cycle — no ladder,
82	no setup commit — skips `-0` and starts at `-1` (its first
83	Work commit). The same holds at every level: a sub-cycle
84	needing no Preparation omits its `.0` (see Nesting). One that
85	grows a Preparation later adds the `0` step without
86	renumbering siblings.
87	
88	Disambiguation:
89	
90	- `-10` — Work commit #10 (final identifier `10`), not a
91	  Preparation.
92	- `-1.0` — Preparation of the `-1` sub-cycle (final
93	  identifier `0`).
94	
95	**Nesting.** Sub-cycles append another level, recursively:
96	
97	- `X.Y.Z-3.0` — Preparation of the `-3` sub-cycle
98	- `X.Y.Z-3.1`, `X.Y.Z-3.2` — its Work
99	- `X.Y.Z-3` — its Close-out
100	- `X.Y.Z-3.1.0` — Preparation of the `-3.1` sub-sub-cycle
101	
102	Bump the version-of-record at the start of each phase, so the
103	active phase is recorded — and, per the preference above,
104	every commit carries a distinct version.
105	
```

13	## Goal
14	
15	A ring buffer for passing typed messages between a producer and
16	a consumer with no copying at the boundary: the producer writes
17	its message directly into buffer memory, the consumer reads it
18	in place. The same layout must work intra-process (threads) and
19	inter-process (shared memory).
20	
21	**Blue-sky goal: ISR-to-ISR messaging.** A stretch target —
22	the same rings carrying messages between *interrupt service
23	routines*, no thread in the loop: N producing ISRs into one
24	consuming ISR, up to N:M for fan-out / consolidation (entities
25	as a graph of ISR nodes,
26	[Execution contexts](#execution-contexts)). Lock-free rings
27	suit ISRs — no lock means no priority inversion — but the
28	gotchas are real:
29	
30	- **No blocking, ever** — an ISR bails, never waits, on Full
31	  or Empty (the `|_| false` policy); backpressure must be drop
32	  or an overflow FIFO, never a spin.
33	- **Something must fire the consumer** — a consuming ISR runs
34	  only when triggered, so an all-ISR system needs a doorbell
35	  (a software interrupt / IPI raised after commit); the ring
36	  does not provide it.
37	- **The CAS floor splits the styles by target** — a shared
38	  MPSC ring's multi-producer claim needs an atomic RMW on
39	  `producer_idx`:
40	  - has-CAS (`target_has_atomic = "32"`, ≥ Cortex-M3) —
41	    hardware CAS provides the claim, nothing extra needed.
42	  - no-CAS (M0 / thumbv6m) — two ways to keep N producers:
43	    - EI/DI-emulated claim: disable interrupts around the
44	      RMW to stand in for the CAS. Keeps the shared ring.
45	      Costs a brief all-interrupt blackout per claim, and is
46	      single-core only — a second core still needs a
47	      hardware spinlock.
48	    - fan-in: each producer gets its own SPSC ring — no
49	      shared claim, no CAS, no EI/DI — and the consumer
50	      polls the N rings.
51	- **Latency is bounded, not constant** — MPSC CAS retries
52	  under nested-ISR contention are bounded, but a hard
53	  real-time budget must account for them.
54	- **panic=abort assumed** — the tombstone/unwind path never
55	  runs in an ISR, so a panicking fill aborts rather than
56	  degrading; fine, but a property to design around.
57	
58	## Terminology
59	
60	The vocabulary the rest of the document builds on, defined
```

128	    the line size fits without padding logic.
129	  - This trades space for isolation (a 16-byte message
130	    occupies a full 64-byte slot) — the right default for
131	    IPC; a packed-slot variant is a possible follow-on.
132	- **Monotonic indices** — producer/consumer indices are
133	  free-running unsigned counters (wrap via masking), so
134	  full/empty are distinguishable without a separate flag or
135	  a sacrificial slot.
136	- **Cache-line separation** — producer- and consumer-owned
137	  indices are padded/aligned to separate cache lines to avoid
138	  false sharing.
139	- **SPSC roles forbid concurrent access, not multiple
140	  owners** — the single-producer and single-consumer contract
141	  is "at most one accessor in an endpoint at a time," a
142	  *serialization* requirement via a serializer/mutex-type
143	  entity, not a fixed thread identity.
144	  How threads and ISRs each satisfy it is
145	  [Execution contexts](#execution-contexts).
146	- **Typed access over untyped slots** — the slot geometry
147	  (M, N) is a property of the buffer, independent of any
148	  message type; a `T` is a zerocopy view into a slot, valid
149	  when `size_of::<T>() <= N` and `align_of::<T>()` divides
150	  the slot alignment (checked at construction or compile
151	  time). Variable-length framed messages are a possible
152	  follow-on, not in this cycle.
153	- **zerocopy, core-only** — depend on zerocopy with default
154	  features compatible with `no_std`; no other runtime
155	  dependencies.
156	
157	### Execution contexts
158	
159	An *execution context* — a thread or an ISR — drives
160	endpoints (a process is only the address-space
161	boundary around one or more of them,
162	[System model](#system-model-overview)). Each satisfies the
163	serialization rule above — one accessor in an endpoint at a
164	time — differently:
165	
166	- **Thread, sole owner of an endpoint** — one thread owns the
167	  endpoint; no sharing, fully lock-free.
168	- **Threads sharing an endpoint** — several take turns under a
169	  mutex:
170	  - producer and consumer sides are symmetric;
171	  - the mutex allows only one thread at a time to use the
172	    endpoint;
```

vc-x1 0.67.0

Dual-repo commit+push+finalize in one resumable command.

Collapses today's manual Commit-Push-Finalize Flow into a
single subcommand with two interactive approval gates and a
state machine with persistent progress so interruptions can
resume without re-doing completed stages.

Stages: preflight (fmt/clippy/test) → review (approve diff)
→ message ($EDITOR / --title+--body, approve text) →
commit-app → commit-claude (skipped if clean) → bookmark-both
→ push-app → finalize-claude. Failures in commit-app /
commit-claude / bookmark-both roll both repos back via
`jj op restore` to the snapshot recorded before commit-app.
After push-app succeeds the remote boundary is crossed and
recovery is forward-only.

Non-interactive use: pass both --title and --body plus --yes
to skip the review gate. Saved state carries title/body
across resumes so only the first invocation needs them.

Usage: vc-x1 push [OPTIONS] [BOOKMARK]

Arguments:
  [BOOKMARK]
          Bookmark to advance in both repos (positional form of `--bookmark`).
          
          Accepting a positional lets the common case read as `vc-x1 push main`
          without the `--bookmark` ceremony; `--bookmark` is kept as an alias
          for scripts and for composition with other args. The two forms
          conflict if both supplied.

Options:
      --bookmark <BOOKMARK>
          Bookmark to advance in both repos (flag form; see positional)

  -V, --version
          Print the `vc-x1 X.Y.Z` banner as the first line, then continue. With
          no subcommand, prints the banner and exits.
          
          Replaces clap's auto-version (which would exit after printing): the
          banner now rides along with normal subcommand execution rather than
          gating it, so scripts can capture the version *and* the command's
          output in one invocation.

      --restart
          Clear any saved state file and start from stage 1

  -v, --verbose...
          Verbose output: -v debug, -vv trace

      --from <STAGE>
          Explicit stage to jump to (advanced / debug use)

          Possible values:
          - preflight:       Run fmt / clippy / test / install / retest
          - review:          Present diff for the first approval gate
          - message:         Compose / edit the commit message; present for
            second gate
```
